Out of 122 recent scientiﬁc papers published at PLDI, ASPLOS,\nISMM, TOPLAS, and TACO, as many as 65 included experimental evaluation that quantiﬁed a performance\nchange using a ratio of execution times. Unfortunately, few of these papers evaluated their results with the\nlevel of rigour that has come to be expected in other experimental sciences. The uncertainty of measured\nresults was largely ignored. Scarcely any of the papers mentioned uncertainty in the ratio of the mean exe-\ncution times, and most did not even mention uncertainty in the two means themselves. Furthermore, most\nof the papers failed to address the non-deterministic execution of computer programs (caused by factors such\nas memory placement, for example), and none addressed non-deterministic compilation (when a compiler\ncreates different binaries from the same sources, which differ in performance, for example again because\nof impact on memory placement). It turns out that the statistical methods presented in the computer sys-\ntems performance evaluation literature for the design and summary of experiments do not readily allow this\neither. This poses a hazard to the repeatability, reproducibility and even validity of quantitative results.\nInspired by statistical methods used in other ﬁelds of science, and building on results in statistics that\ndid not make it to introductory textbooks, we present a statistical model that allows us both to quantify\nuncertainty in the ratio of (execution time) means and to design experiments with a rigorous treatment of\nthose multiple sources of non-determinism that might impact measured performance. Better still, under\nour framework summaries can be as simple as “system A is faster than system B by 5.5% ± 2.5%, with\n95% conﬁdence”, a more natural statement than those derived from typical current practice, which are often\nmisinterpreted.\nCategories and Subject Descriptors: D.2.8 [Software Engineering]: Metrics—Performance measures; D.3.4\n[Programming Languages]: Processors—Run-time environments\nGeneral Terms: Experimentation, Measurement, Performance\nAdditional Key Words and Phrases: statistical methods, random effects, effect size\n1. INTRODUCTION\nQuantiﬁcation of performance change is a common task in experimental computer sci-\nence. Out of all 122 papers published in ASPLOS, ISMM, PLDI, TACO and TOPLAS in\n2011 up to 2nd August, 65 included empirical evaluation that quantiﬁed performance\nchange by giving the ratio of execution times (for example, speed-ups of optimisations,\nor overheads of new techniques). Quantiﬁcation of performance change is also part of\nthe software development process (for example, detecting performance regressions),\nboth open-source and commercial.\nExecution times for quantiﬁcation are obtained by running benchmark applications\nusing the two systems to compare. Computer systems are becoming more complex and\nincreasingly resemble the direct products of nature observed by physicists and natu-\nral scientists — an overwhelming number of factors inﬂuence performance. Some are\nunknown, some are out of the experimenter’s control, and some are non-deterministic.\nRepeated executions of the same benchmark on the same system, even when the exper-\nimenter does everything possible to enforce the same conditions, always report slightly\ndifferent execution times. This is even more pronounced if the same experiment is re-\npeated by an independent experimenter using different equipment. Thus, for credible\nAuthor’s addresses: T. Kalibera (R.E. Jones), School of Computing, University of Kent, Canterbury, CT2 7NF,\nUK.\nA preliminary version of a portion of this work was presented at the Third European Performance Engineering\nWorkshop.\narXiv:2007.10899v1  [s\n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 32000:36000]\ner detail in Section 3.2). Jain\n[1991] and Lilja [2000] suggest using Student’s t-test for unequal variances (or the cor-\nresponding interval). Georges et al. [2007] additionally recommend (an interval based\non) the z-test for sample sizes over 30. Georges et al. also suggest working with sample\nmeans of measurements from individual executions, instead of with the measurements\nthemselves. The corresponding test/interval is hence only for these sample means. An\nalternative solution that is sometimes used, but not part of their recommendation, is\nto join all the measurements from the different executions and treat them as coming\nfrom a one-level experiment.\nLilja [2000] and particularly Jain [1991] then describe analysis of variance (ANOVA)\nmethods to summarize experiments where multiple ﬁxed factors are varied (i.e. pro-\ncessor, memory size, etc.). Georges et al. [2007] summarizes also some of the ANOVA\nmethods from [Lilja 2000], but does not recommend it for use in (Java) performance\n\n\n=== PDF page 9 ===\nQuantifying Performance Changes with Effect Size Conﬁdence Intervals\n9\nevaluations as the outputs are too hard to understand and doing all the measurements\nto provide the inputs is too time consuming. We do not address these types of factors\n(‘ﬁxed effects’) in this work, but our method should be extensible to do so.\nThe summarisation method that Georges et al. [2008] recommends for the replay\ncompilation design leads to a test/interval based on paired t-test. The inputs are dif-\nferences of means obtained from the old and new systems, averaging over multiple ex-\necutions with a given compilation plan. We do not address paired comparisons in this\nwork, but most of our criticism of the recommended quantiﬁcation using the test/inter-\nval we mention later applies to it as well.\n3. WHAT IS WRONG WITH THE CURRENTLY PROPOSED METHODS\nEven though the current recommended best practice for quantiﬁcation of performance\nchange by [Jain 1991], [Lilja 2000], and [Georges et al. 2007] is ahead of the pitiful\npractice in the ﬁeld, it has a number of problems.\n3.1. Experiment Design\nRepetition of executions (and measurements) is not enough. It is necessary to include\nall inherently random factors in the experiment, and hence to repeat experiments at\nlevel(s) even higher than execution of the benchmark. For example, randomized com-\npilation necessitates both compilation and running the experiments for different bina-\nries. We have found this to be essential in our earlier work with the GNU C++ Com-\npiler when benchmarking CORBA middleware and when benchmarking applications\nwithin the Mono platform [Kalibera and Tuma 2006]. The empirical evaluation in this\npaper conﬁrms this need on the Mono platform. Furthermore, we need to randomize\nand include additional (non-random) factors that it does not make sense to ﬁx. These\nfactors can arise from the operating system or the language environment. Mytkowicz\net al. [2009] provide evidence that unexpected factors such as the size of UNIX process\nenvironment or the linking order can impact performance. Gu et al. [2004] observed\na performance impact of the names of identiﬁers in the source code. It is obvious that\nmany additional factors of this kind have not yet been discovered to have signiﬁcant\nperformance impact, so they cannot be really ﬁxed in experiments. Sufﬁcient random-\nization, called for by Mytkowicz et al. [2009], is hence necessary. Curtsinger and Berger\n[2012] provide a randomization tool applicable to applications studied in [Mytkowicz\net al. 2009].\nNote in particular that randomization of identiﬁer names or linking order leads in\nturn again to randomized compilation/building. Repeating compilation or repeating at\nan even higher level can be very expensive, because large systems take long to compile.\nRepeating too many times is a waste of resources, but repeating too few times under-\nmines repeatability and validity. Hence, a good experiment design method should also\nallow the researcher to \n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 48000:52000]\nting a conﬁdence interval for a metric that measures the difference in two\nsystems is a known concept in statistics. Such a metric is called the effect size and\nhence the effect size conﬁdence interval. Effect size conﬁdence intervals have been pro-\nposed for quantiﬁcation as a replacement for signiﬁcance methods in psychology [Co-\nhen 1994], education [Coe 2002], medicine, bio-medicine, and biology [Nakagawa and\nCuthill 2007]. The change in statistical methods is not smooth, but evidence can be\nfound that effect size is already being used, at least to some extent, in medicine [Bland\nand Altman 2000] and psychology [Dunleavy et al. 2006]. To support the change, the\nuse of effect size has sometimes been made an ofﬁcial requirement (Hill and Thompson\n[2005] list 23 journals mostly in psychology and education that require reporting effect\nsize, and the American Psychological Association [2001] requires it in its Publication\nManual).\n2In statistical texts, one can say also ‘n factors’, but we refrain from doing so here to avoid confusion with\nthe informal meaning of a ‘factor’ used throughout the text. In our text, a ‘factor’ refers to a technical/real\ncause that may impact performance and a ‘way’ refers to a statistical model of (some of) such causes.\n\n\n=== PDF page 13 ===\nQuantifying Performance Changes with Effect Size Conﬁdence Intervals\n13\nThere are different metrics used for measure the effect size in different ﬁelds and\none of them is the ratio of means. There are statistical methods for interval construc-\ntion, although they did not make it to introductory textbooks (more information and\nreferences can be found in [Beyene and Moineddin 2005; Schaarschmidt 2007; Dilba\net al. 2007]). From several available methods, we have a closer look at two. One is\nbased on statistical simulation (bootstrap) [Davison and Hinkley 1997]. The other is\nbased on Fieller’s theorem [Fieller 1954], which gives a conﬁdence interval for the ratio\nof means of normally distributed variables. We show how both methods can be applied\nin the experimental design we propose, and empirically evaluate them.\n4.3. Related Efforts\nSome of the things we propose here have been proposed earlier in computer science,\nbut have not been widely adopted.\nA bootstrap-based method for construction of conﬁdence intervals is being used in\nthe Haskell community, supported by the Criterion benchmarking library [O’Sullivan\n2009]. The library is for measuring performance of one system only in single-level\nexperiments. Apart from using robust methods it has also the advantage of detecting\noutliers and auto-corellation of the data. The tool is based on [Boyer 2008].\nConﬁdence interval for speed-up has been proposed by Luo and John [2004] in\nthe context of processor simulation. The uncertainty does not come from random ef-\nfects, but rather from random sub-sampling – only randomly chosen execution inter-\nvals from the whole application execution are simulated, the same intervals in both\nof the systems to compare. The conﬁdence interval proposed for this problem comes\nfrom [Cochran 1977], it is a parametric interval based on asymptotic normality of not\nonly the means, but also their ratio, which is only possible for large samples. The\nmethod by Fieller [1954] that we use in this work is more general.\n5. OUTLINE OF THE NEW METHOD\nThe rest of our paper includes a detailed description of our quantiﬁcation method and\nits empirical evaluation based on real benchmark results. We offer two alternative\ndescriptions of our method. Section 6 provides a guide for practitioners, while Sec-\ntion 7 is a variant for scientists with some statistical background. Section 6 describes\nthe method in practical terms and with recommendations on how it can be used. Sec-\ntion 7 formulates the method in statistical terms, gives the assumptions and necessary\nproofs, and discusses further alternatives. We use the terminology common in the ﬁeld.\nIn statistical terms, the core of the method is our statistical m\n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 68000:72000]\n demonstrate the method. Variances at the\nhighest level are simply variances of means (of binaries, in our case). For the old sys-\ntem, the mean for the ﬁrst binary is 7.8, for the second 12.3 and for the third 11.5 (all\nnumbers rounded to one decimal place). We would\nthen calculate the ratio of means of these replicates (the ﬁrst of the new system over\nthe ﬁrst of the old system, the second of the new over the second of the old, etc.). From\nthese ratios, we would select the respective quantiles.\n7. FOR SCIENTISTS: THE NEW METHOD AND THE STATISTICS BEHIND IT\nIn this section, we formulate the method in statistical terms, give its assumptions\nand provide proofs. We also discuss alternatives. In the description of the statis-\ntics behind our method, we assume the execution time of the operation of interest\nis a continuous random variable, Y . The range of Y is a subrange of real numbers:\n∃BCET, WCET ∈R : P(Y < BCET) = P(Y > WCET) = 0, where BCET is the best-\ncase execution time and WCET is the worst-case execution time. We assume that the\nexpectation of Y exists and that its variance is ﬁnite, E(Y ) = µ and var(Y ) = σ2\n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 108000:116000]\n both types are biased. Estimators for a broad range of random and ﬁxed effects\nmodels can be found in [Searle et al. 1992].\nIn the rest of this section, we will calculate the expectations of S2\ni . We start with the\nlemmas we will use.\nLEMMA 7.8.\nLet z = 1\nn\nPn\ni=1 zi. Then Pn\ni=1 (zi −z)2 =\n\u0000Pn\ni=1 z2\ni\n\u0001\n−n (z)2 .\nLEMMA 7.9.
